Theory and Algorithms for Diffusion Processes on Riemannian Manifolds Thanks: Part of this work was done when JZ was with MIT.
\name Xiang Cheng \email chengx@mit.edu

Abstract
We study geometric stochastic differential equations (SDEs) and their approximations on Riemannian manifolds. In particular, we introduce a simple new construction of geometric SDEs, using which we provide the first (to our knowledge) non-asymptotic bound on the error of the geometric Euler-Murayama discretization. We then bound the distance between the exact SDE and a discrete geometric random walk, where the noise can be non-Gaussian; this analysis is useful for using geometric SDEs to model naturally occurring discrete non-Gaussian stochastic processes. Our results provide convenient new tools for studying MCMC algorithms that adopt non-standard noise distributions.



1
Introduction

S
tochastic differential equations (SDEs) offer a powerful formalism for studying diffusion processes, Brownian motion, and algorithms for sampling and optimization. We study the following
geometric stochastic differential equation
:

dx(t)=\beta(x(t))dt+dB^{g}_{t},

(1.1)

that evolves on a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
. Similar to its Euclidean counterpart, here too
\beta:x\to T_{x}M
is a drift (
T_{x}M
is the tangent space at
x\in M
); while
dB^{g}_{t}
denotes standard Brownian motion on
M

(
Hsu 2002
, Ch. 3)
. The notation in (
1.1
) is a convenient shorthand; for a more precise version see
(
Hsu 2002
, Thm. 1.3.6)
).

Geometric SDEs such as (
1.1
) play a crucial role in the design and analysis of MCMC algorithms
(
Girolami and Calderhead 2011
;
Patterson and Teh 2013
)
that have had much success in solving Bayesian problems on statistical manifolds. These SDEs also directly relate to the tasks of sampling and optimization on manifolds, where often a Lie group structure helps capture symmetries (e.g., the Grassmann manifold,
\text{SO}(n)
,
O(n)
, etc.)
(
Moitra and Risteski 2020
;
Piggott and Solo 2016
)
. Moreover, in some settings MCMC algorithms with respect to carefully chosen metrics can be faster than their Euclidean counterparts
(
Lee and Vempala 2018
;
Chewi et al. 2020
)
.

A basic scheme for simulating the SDE (
1.1
) is
Geometric Euler-Murayama

(
Piggott and Solo 2016
;
Muniz et al. 2021
)
. This method performs the manifold-valued iteration:

x_{k+1}=\Exp_{x_{k}}\bigl(\delta\beta(x_{k})+\sqrt{\delta}\zeta_{k}\bigr),

(1.2)

where
\Exp_{x}
denotes the exponential map for
M
, and
\zeta_{k}
is a standard Gaussian with respect to
any
orthonormal basis of
T_{x_{k}}M
. It has long been known that the discrete-time process (
1.2
) converges to the SDE (
1.1
) in the limit
(
Gangolli 1964
;
Jørgensen 1975
;
Hsu 2002
)
.

While several authors
(
Piggott and Solo 2016
;
Muniz et al. 2021
;
Li and Erdogdu 2020
;
Moitra and Risteski 2020
)
have analyzed discretization of (
1.1
) in specific settings, much less is known about the
nonasymptotic
error between the SDE (
1.1
) and its Euler-Murayama discretization on general Riemannian manifolds. Moreover, equally important is a study of other discretizations that approximate (
1.1
) with non-Gaussian position-dependent noise, as these other discretizations model a richer family of stochastic processes, and may be computationally more advantageous than (
1.2
).

Main Goals and Contributions of This Paper

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

Goal I.
Our first goal is to quantify the discretization error of the Geometric Euler-Murayama scheme (
1.2
), in terms of the stepsize
\delta
and intrinsic quantities such as the curvature of
M
. Unlike Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it is not always clear how one might sample from the endpoint of a geometric Brownian motion. (On certain special manifolds, exact sampling is possible, e.g.
Li and Erdogdu 2020
). Our discretization analysis in Lemma
4
guarantees that (
1.2
), which is amenable to efficient computation, is a good approximation to the SDE (
1.1
).

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

Main theoretical contributions associated with Goal I.

–

We first construct the exact SDE (
1.1
) as the limit of a family of increasingly finer Euler-Murayama sequences. These sequences are carefully coupled by our construction which shows that within a short time the trajectory of (
1.2
) is roughly parallel to (
1.1
). A significant challenge here lies in analyzing discretizations of geometric Brownian motion and controlling the rotation of orthonormal frames as they get parallel transported along the diffusion path.

–

Next, using this construction in Lemma
4
, we show that the error between a single
\delta
-step of Euler-Murayama (
1.2
) and (
1.1
) evolved over a period of
\delta
is
O{(\delta^{3/2})}
; this error bound matches the one-step error of Euler-Murayama in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Importantly, our analysis does not rely on properties of a particular embedding, and hence our error bounds do not depend on extrinsic quantities such as Christoffel symbols.

–

Finally, in Theorem
1
we bound the
W_{1}
distance (with respect to the Riemannian distance on
M
) between (
1.1
) and the Euler-Murayama scheme, for all time
t
. To sample from a distribution
\varepsilon
-close to the invariant distribution of (
1.1
), Theorem
1
requires
\tilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2})
steps of (
1.2
), matching the best known iteration complexity of Langevin MCMC in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

Goal II.
Our second goal is to extend the previous error bound to the stochastic process

x_{k+1}=\Exp_{x_{k}}\bigl(\delta\beta(x_{k})+\sqrt{\delta}\xi_{k}(x_{k})\bigr),

(1.3)

where
\xi_{k}
are i.i.d. samples of a random vector field
\xi
. In contrast to (
1.2
), process (
1.3
) permits the noise to be non-Gaussian and position dependent. We are interested in cases when
\xi(x)
is zero-mean with an identity covariance, but its distribution is not Gaussian and might not even be invariant under parallel transport. Our goal is to understand the process (
1.3
), and how it approximates the SDE (
1.1
) (see Theorem
2
). The main motivation behind this goal is that (
1.3
) can capture certain naturally occuring stochastic processes; one example is the recent interest in modelling SGD noise as Brownian motion over the manifold embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose metric tensor is given by the inverse of the covariance matrix of the stochastic gradient
(
Li et al. 2021
;
Cheng et al. 2020
;
Pesme et al. 2021
)
. Moreover, (
1.3
) can be computationally better than the Geometric Euler-Murayama scheme—e.g., when computing the metric tensor is expensive but generating samples with the right covariance is easy.

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

Main theoretical contributions associated with Goal II.

–

We provide a bound on the
W_{1}
distance between
K
steps of (
1.3
) and SDE (
1.1
) taken over a time interval of
K\delta
(Lemma
5
). Specifically, if
x(t)
is as in (
1.1
) and
\{x_{k}\}_{k}
is the discrete sequence obtained via (
1.3
), then Lemma
5
shows that for sufficiently large
K
,
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}(x(K\delta),x_{K})}\right]\leq\tilde{O}(\delta^{1/6})
.

–

Our proof approximates
K
steps of (
1.3
) by a
K
-step-random-walk in
T_{x_{0}}M
. Theorem
2
bounds the error due to this approximation. The main idea in analyzing this error is to posit a suitable second-order ODE in
T_{x_{0}}M
arising from the Jacobi Equation (Lemma
69
). This lemma may be useful for analyzing so-called “trivializations” of other stochastic algorithms.

–

Enroute, we also prove an extension of the classic quantitative CLT; this result bounds the distance between
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}
and
\mathcal{N}(0,I)
, and allows
\xi_{i}
to depend on
\xi_{0},\ldots,\xi_{i-1}
.

2
Preliminaries: notation and key assumptions

We assume some background in Riemannian geometry, and freely use standard notation; we refer the reader to
(
Jost 2008
;
Lee 2006
;
Petersen 2006
)
for an in depth treatment. Readers may also find some works on Riemannian optimization useful as additional context:
(
Bacák 2014
;
Udriste 2013
;
Absil et al. 2009
;
Zhang and Sra 2016
;
Boumal 2022
)
.

We use capital letters to denote an ordered orthonormal basis at some tangent space, e.g.,
F
is a basis of
T_{x}M
. We use superscripts to index vectors in the basis, e.g., a basis
F
is an ordered tuple
(F^{1},\ldots,F^{d})
of vectors in
T_{x}M
. We use bold lowercase letters
\mathbf{v}
to denote Euclidean vectors, and use
\mathbf{v}\circ F
as shorthand for
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{v}_{i}F^{i}
. A distribution that we will see frequently in this paper is the one given by
{\bm{\xi}}\circ E^{x}
, where
{\bm{\xi}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
is a random vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
x\in M
and
E^{x}
is an orthonormal basis of
T_{x}M
. We use
\mathcal{N}_{x}{(0,I)}
to denote the distribution of
{\bm{\xi}}\circ E^{x}
. One can verify that
\mathcal{N}_{x}{(0,I)}
does not depend on the choice of basis
E^{x}
. We use
\Pi_{a}(v)
to denote projection onto the (Riemannian)
a
-ball, so that
\Pi_{a}(v)=v
if
{\left\|v\right\|}\leq a
, and
\Pi_{a}(v)=\frac{av}{{\left\|v\right\|}}
, otherwise. We use
\mathrm{d}(\cdot,\cdot)
to denote the Riemannian distance on
M
. We use
\nabla
to denote the Levi Civita connection. Given
x,y\in M
and
v\in T_{x}M
, we use
\Gamma_{x}^{y}v
to denote parallel transport of
v
from
x
to
y
. Given a general curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
, we will also use
\Gamma_{\gamma(t)}v
to denote the parallel transport of
v
from
\gamma(0)
to
\gamma(1)
, along
\gamma
.

Our first assumption is a natural generalization of the dissipativity condition in the Euclidean setting. It assumes that the drift traps the variable within a bounded region.

Assumption 1
.

We call a vector field
\beta

(m,q,\mathcal{R})
-
distant-dissipative
if there exist constants
\mathcal{R},m,q\geq 0
such that, for all
x,y
satisfying
\mathrm{d}{(x,y)}\geq\mathcal{R}
, there exists a minimizing geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
with
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=y
, such that (here
\Gamma_{y}^{x}
denotes parallel transport from
T_{y}M
to
T_{x}M
along
\gamma
) the inequality

\displaystyle\bm{\langle}\Gamma_{y}^{x}{\beta(y)-\beta(x)},\gamma^{\prime}(0)\bm{\rangle}\leq-m\mathrm{d}{(x,y)}^{2},

holds, and for all
x,y
satisfying
\mathrm{d}(x,y)\leq\mathcal{R}
, there exists a minimizing geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
with
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma(1)=y
, such that we have instead the inequality

\displaystyle\bm{\langle}\Gamma_{y}^{x}{\beta(y)-\beta(x)},\gamma^{\prime}(0)\bm{\rangle}\leq q\mathrm{d}{(x,y)}^{2}.

We then bound the smoothness of the drift and of the Riemannian curvature, before we can carry out the promised nonasymptotic analysis.

Assumption 2
.

A vector field
\beta
is
L_{\beta}
-Lipschitz if, for all
x\in M
and all
v\in T_{x}M
,
{\left\|\nabla_{v}\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}{\left\|v\right\|}
.

Assumption 3
.

We assume that the manifold
M
has Riemannian curvature tensor
R
that satisfies for all
x\in M
, and for all
u,v,w,z\in T_{x}M
, the bound
\bm{\left\langle}R(u,v)w,z\bm{}\leq L_{R}{\left\|u\right\|}{\left\|v\right\|}{\left\|w\right\|}{\left\|z\right\|}
for some
L_{R}\geq 0
.

Assumption 4
.

We assume that for all
x\in M
,
u,u\in T_{x}M
,
\Ric(u,u)\geq-L_{\Ric}
, for some
L_{\Ric}\geq 0
.

Although Assumption
4
is implied by Assumption
3
with
L_{\Ric}=d\cdot L_{R}
, we state them separately as sometimes
L_{\Ric}\ll d\cdot L_{R}
.

3
A Construction for SDEs on Manifolds and Langevin MCMC

In this section, we bound the error incurred by the Euler-Murayama discretization. To that end, we introduce a different construction of geometric SDEs. Specifically, we present a family of processes
\{x^{i}(t)\}_{i\geq 0}
(defined in (
3.2
) below), where marginally, each
x^{i}(t)
corresponds to a Euler-Murayama discretization with stepsize
\delta^{i}\propto 2^{-i}
. We verify in Lemma
1
that
x^{i}(t)
has an almost-sure limit
x(t)
, and in Lemma
2
that this limit is equivalent to the distribution in (
1.1
) as it has the same generator.

We choose to use our construction of SDEs instead of the usual approach (e.g., Chapter 2 of
Hsu 2002
), as it enables us to easily analyze the discretization error:
aAdjacent pairs of processes
x^{i}(t)
and
x^{i+1}(t)
are coupled using the manifold analog of synchronous coupling (see Remark
1
), and the distance between
x^{0}(t)
and the limit
x(t)
, can be bound by summing (over
i
) the pairwise distances between
x^{i}(t)
and
x^{i+1}(t)
, which are small due to synchronous coupling. We hope that readers unfamiliar with Riemannian geometry, but familiar with stochastic analysis in Euclidean space, will find our construction more accessible than the standard approach, as we only use the basic Jacobi equation and do not invoke non-constructive embeddings of manifolds into
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

With the above construction, Theorem
1
bounds the difference between a manifold SDE and its Euler-Murayama discretization. We are now ready to present our construction in Section
3.1
.

3.1
Construction of Manifold SDE on Dyadic Points

To bound the discretization error, we first characterize the manifold SDE (
1.1
) as the limit of a family of random processes. Let
x_{0}\in M
be an initial point and
E^{1},\ldots,E^{d}
an orthonormal basis of
T_{x^{0}}
. Let
\mathbf{B}(t)
denote a standard Brownian Motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
T\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Define the initialization

\displaystyle x^{0}_{0}=x_{0},\qquad E^{0,j}_{0}=E^{j},

\displaystyle x^{0}_{1}=\Exp_{x^{0}_{0}}(T\beta(x^{0}_{0})+{{(\mathbf{B}{(T)}-\mathbf{B}{(0)})}}\circ E^{0}_{0}).

For any
i\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, let
\delta^{i}:=2^{-i}T
. We define points
x^{i}_{k}\in M
and vectors
E^{i,j}_{k}
in suitable tangent spaces for all
i
, all
k\in\{0,\ldots,\nicefrac{{T}}{{\delta^{i}}}\}
, and all
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
. Our construction is inductive: Suppose we have already defined
x^{i}_{k}
and
E^{i,j}_{k}
for some
i
, for all
k\in\{0,\ldots,\nicefrac{{T}}{{\delta^{i}}}\}
, and for all
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,d\right\}
. Then, for
i+1
, we define for all
k=\{0,\ldots,\nicefrac{{T}}{{\delta^{i}}}\}
the updated values

\displaystyle x^{i+1}_{0}:=x_{0},\qquad E^{i+1,j}_{0}:=E^{j},

\displaystyle x^{i+1}_{2k+1}:=\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(\delta^{i+1}\beta{(x^{i+1}_{2k})}+{{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+1)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{(2k\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ E^{i+1}_{2k})},

\displaystyle E^{i+1}_{2k+1}:=\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k+1}}{(E^{i+1}_{2k})},

\displaystyle x^{i+1}_{2k+2}:=\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k+1}}{(\delta^{i+1}\beta{(x^{i+1}_{2k+1})}+{{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+2)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{({(2k+1)}\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ E^{i+1}_{2k+1})},

\displaystyle E^{i+1}_{2k+2}:=\Gamma_{x^{i}_{k+1}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k+2}}{(E^{i}_{k+1})}.

(3.1)

The above display defines points
x^{i+1}_{k}
for all
k=\left\{0,\ldots,\nicefrac{{T}}{{\delta^{i+1}}}\right\}
. For the parallel transport, if the minimizing geodesic is not unique, any arbitrary choice will do. Finally, we also define for any
i
, and for any
t\in[k\delta^{i},(k+1)\delta^{i})
, the point
x^{i}(t)
to be the “linear interpolation” of
x^{i}_{k}
and
x^{i}_{k+1}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle x^{i}(t):=\Exp_{x^{i}_{k}}\bigl((t-k\delta^{i})\beta{(x^{i}_{k})}+{\tfrac{(t-k\delta^{i})}{\delta^{i}}{(\mathbf{B}{(t)}-\mathbf{B}{(k\delta^{i})})}}\circ E^{i}_{k}\bigr).

(3.2)

We verify that
x^{i}(t)
defined above coincides with
x^{i}_{k}
defined in (
3.1
) when
t=k\delta^{i}
for some
k
. In general, we let

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B},i)

(3.3)

denote the solution to the interpolated process in (
3.2
), initialized at
x_{0}=x
. We also let

\displaystyle\Phi(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B}):=\lim_{i\to\infty}\overline{\Phi}(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B},i)

(3.4)

We verify in Lemma
1
that
\Phi(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B},i)
exists, and in Lemma
2
that
\Phi(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B})
is equal in distribution to (
1.1
).

Remark 1
.

The choice of basis
E^{i}_{k}
in (
3.1
) can be seen as a combination of “synchronous coupling” and “rolling without slipping” (see Chapter 2 of
Hsu 2002
). In particular,
E^{i+1}_{2k}:=\Gamma_{x^{i}_{k}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(E^{i}_{k})}
corresponds to “synchronous coupling”—the step from
x^{i+1}_{2k}
to
x^{i+1}_{2k+1}
is (roughly) parallel to the step from
x^{i}_{k}
to
x^{i}_{k+1}
. On the other hand,
E^{i+1}_{2k+1}:=\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k+1}}{(E^{i+1}_{2k})}
corresponds to “rolling without slipping”—the step from
x^{i+1}_{2k+1}
to
x^{i+1}_{2k+2}
is with respect to an orthonormal frame that is parallel-transported from
x^{i+1}_{2k}
to
x^{i+1}_{2k+1}
.

After constructing
x^{i}(t)
via (
3.2
), the next step is to understand its limit as
i\to\infty
. We will soon see that this limit is equivalent to the manifold SDE (
1.1
). Lemma
1
shows that this limit exists almost surely, uniformly over
[0,T]
, if
\beta
satisfies
Assumption
2
.

Lemma 1
.

Let
x\in M
be some initial point and
E
an orthonormal basis of
T_{x}M
. Let
\mathbf{B}(t)
be a Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; and
\beta(x)
a vector field satisfying Assumption
2
. Let
T\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, for
t\in[0,T]
and let
x^{i}(t)
be constructed as per (
3.2
).
Then with probability 1, there is a limit
x(t)
such that for all
\varepsilon
, there exists an integer
N
such that for all
i\geq N
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x(t))}\leq\varepsilon.

We defer the proof of Lemma
1
to Section
A
, and proceed to verify that the limit
x(t)
indeed
defines an SDE.

Lemma 2
.

For any
T
, for
t\in[0,T]
, let
x(t)
be the limit ensured by Lemma
1
. Then
x(t)
is diffusion process generated by the operator
L
whose action on any smooth function
f
is given by
Lf=\bm{\left\langle}\nabla f,\beta\bm{}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta(f)
, where
\Delta
denotes the Laplace Beltrami operator.

Lemma
2
follows from
(
Gangolli 1964
;
Jørgensen 1975
)
. We provide a proof in Appendix
A.2
for completeness. By Theorem 1.3.7 of
(
Hsu 2002
)
, this implies that
x(t)
exactly corresponds to (
1.1
); moreover, its distribution does not depend on the choice of
E
,
\mathbf{B}
, or time interval
T
.

Next, present a result that quantifies the evolution of distance between two trajectories of (
1.1
):

Lemma 3
.

Assume
\beta
is
(m,q,\mathcal{R})
-distant dissipative as per Assumption
1
, and that is also satisfies Assumption
2
. Further assume that
m\geq 2L_{\Ric}
and
q+L_{\Ric}\geq 0
. Let
x_{0},y_{0}\in M
, while
E^{x}
,
E^{y}
are some orthonormal bases at
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
, respectively. Let
\mathbf{B}^{x}
and
\mathbf{B}^{y}
be two Brownian motions. Let
T
be some positive time. Finally, let
x(t)=\Phi(t;x_{0},E^{x},\beta,\mathbf{B}^{x})
and
y(t)=\Phi(t;y_{0},E^{y},\beta,\mathbf{B}^{y})
be as defined in (
3.4
).
Then there exists a Lyapunov function
f
, such that
f(r)\geq\frac{1}{2}\exp{(-{(q+L_{\Ric})}\mathcal{R}^{2}/2)}r
and
{\left|f^{\prime}(r)\right|}\leq 1
, such that

\displaystyle\inf_{\Omega}\mathbb{E}\left[{f{(\mathrm{d}{(x(T),y(T))})}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\exp{(-\alpha T)}f{(\mathrm{d}{(x_{0},y_{0})})},

where
\Omega
denotes all couplings between
\mathbf{B}^{x}
and
\mathbf{B}^{y}
, and
\alpha:=\min\left\{\frac{m}{16},\frac{1}{2\mathcal{R}^{2}}\right\}\cdot\exp{(-\frac{1}{2}{(q+L_{\Ric})}\mathcal{R}^{2})}
.

Lemma
3
(with minor variation) was first presented in
Eberle 2016
. The Lyapunov function
f
is also taken from
Eberle 2016
and its form is given in (
2
). Since
f(r)\in[\frac{1}{2}e^{-{(q+L_{\Ric})}\mathcal{R}^{2}/2}r,r]
, Lemma
3
shows that two trajectores of (
1.1
) (with different initialization) can be coupled in a way such that their distance contracts. This very useful for showing that approximations of the SDE (
1.1
), such as the geometric Euler-Murayama discretization, also converge in Wasserstein distance to something close to (
1.1
).

Remark 2
.

The proof of Lemma
3
is deferred to Appendix
B.3
as it is rather involved. Our proof technique is based on Kendall-Cranston coupling, which we describe in Appendix
B.1
. The Lyapunov function
f
is taken from
(
Eberle 2016
)
, its exact form corresponds is given in (
2
) in Appendix
B.2
. Lemma
3
is not new. For instance, Theorem 6.6.2 of
(
Hsu 2002
)
+ Itô’s Lemma, applied to the Lyapunov function
f
will give the same bound. Nonetheless, we provide a proof of Lemma
3
for completeness.

Finally, accoutered with the above construction and the corresponding distance bounds, we are ready to describe our main result for this section.

3.2
Langevin MCMC on Riemannian Manifolds

Our first main result is Theorem
1
on Riemannian Langevin MCMC. It bounds the
W_{1}
distance between iterates, and implies that iterates generated by Langevin MCMC converge to the stationary distribution when the discretization step is small enough.

Theorem 1

(Langevin MCMC on Manifolds under distant dissipativity)
.

Assume the manifold
M
satisfies Assumptions
3
and
4
. Additionally, assume that point 1 from Assumption
5
holds for some
L_{R}^{\prime}
(this last assumption is for analytical convenience; it does not show up in the quantitative bounds). Let
\beta
be a vector field satisfying Assumptions
1
and
2
; assume in addition that
m\geq 2L_{\Ric}
and that
q+L_{\Ric}\geq 0
. Let
x^{*}
be a point with
\beta(x^{*})=0
and let
x_{0}
be a point satisfying
\mathrm{d}{(x_{0},x^{*})}\leq\mathcal{R}
. Let
K\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be some iteration number and
\delta
be some stepsize. Let
y_{k}
denote the exact geometric SDE, defined by
y_{k+1}=\Phi{(\delta,y_{0},{E^{k}},\beta,\mathbf{B}_{k})}
, where
{E^{k}}
is some orthonormal basis at
y_{k}
,
\mathbf{B}_{k}
is a
\mathbb{R}^{d}
Brownian Motion, and
\Phi
is as defined in (
3.4
). Let
x_{k}
denote the Euler Murayama discretization of
\Phi
, defined by
x_{k+1}=\Exp_{x_{k}}{(\delta\beta(x_{k})+\sqrt{\delta}\xi_{k}(x_{k}))}
, where
\xi_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}_{x_{k}}{(0,I)}
.

Then, there exists a constant
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}=poly{(L_{\beta}^{\prime},d,L_{R},\mathcal{R},\frac{1}{m},\log K)}
, such that if
\delta\leq\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{0}}
, then there is a coupling between
x_{K}
and
y_{K}
satisfying the distance bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y_{K},x_{K})}}\right]\leq e^{-\alpha K\delta+{(q+L_{Ric})}\mathcal{R}^{2}/2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y_{0},x_{0})}}\right]+\exp{({(q+L_{Ric})}\mathcal{R}^{2})}\cdot\tilde{O}{(\delta^{1/2})}.

\tilde{O}
hides polynomial dependence on
L_{\beta}^{\prime},d,L_{R},\mathcal{R},\frac{1}{m},\log K,\log\frac{1}{\delta}
, and
\alpha:=\min\left\{\frac{m}{16},\frac{1}{2\mathcal{R}^{2}}\right\}\cdot e^{{(-\frac{1}{2}{(q+L_{\Ric})}\mathcal{R}^{2})}}
.

We defer the proof of Theorem
1
to Section
A.4
, where we state the explicit expressions for
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.

3.2.1
Discussion of Theorem
1

One can view the
x_{k}
in Theorem
1
as the manifold version of an Euler-Murayama discretization of the SDE described by
\Phi
. To ensure that the distance between the
x_{K}
and
y_{K}
is bounded by
\varepsilon
, one needs
K=\tilde{O}{(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}})}
steps with stepsize
\tilde{O}{(\varepsilon^{2})}
. This result matches the rate for unadjusted Langevin MCMC on Euclidean space, see e.g.,
(
Durmus and Moulines 2017
)
.

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

Implementing Langevin MCMC.
A step
x_{k}
in Theorem
1
involves computing an exponential map, which may be expensive. But taking a step in a random Gaussian direction is still usually much easier than sampling a Brownian motion on a manifold, even ignoring the effect of
\beta
.

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

Distant Dissipativity.
This assumption ensures that the manifold SDE (and its approximation) stays within a ball of radius
\mathcal{R}^{*}
. Let
\beta
satisfy Assumption
2
; let
g:M\to\mathbb{R}
have a Hessian that is 1-strongly-convex outside a ball of radius
\mathcal{R}_{g}
around
x^{*}
, then
\beta+2L_{\beta}^{\prime}g
satisfies Assumption
1
with
m=L_{\beta}^{\prime},q=L_{\beta}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{R}=2\mathcal{R}_{g}
. This assumption is commonly used when analyzing nonconvex Langevin MCMC in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as well
(
Eberle 2016
;
Gorham et al. 2019
;
Cheng et al. 2020
)
.

The requirement
q+L_{\Ric}\geq 0
is without loss of generality. If
q+L_{\Ric}\leq 0
, we can take
\mathcal{R}=0
and
m^{\prime}=\max\left\{m,-(q+L_{\Ric})\right\}
, and verify that
\beta
satisfies Assumption
1
with
(m^{\prime},q,0)
. The mixing rate is then
\alpha=\frac{m^{\prime}}{16}
, which corresponds to the easy setting, e.g. when
\beta
is the gradient of a function satisfying the
CD(0,\infty)
condition. The hard case is when
q+L_{\Ric}
is positive (e.g., when
\beta
is the gradient of a concave function, or when the manifold is negatively curved); here the mixing rate
\alpha\propto\exp{(-{(q+L_{\Ric})}\mathcal{R}^{2})}
is exponentially small, and this dependence is generally unavoidable.

3.2.2
Proof Sketch of Theorem
1
and Theoretical Contributions

There are three key ingredients that make up the proof:

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

I. Discretization Error Bound.
The first part of our analysis bounds the Wasserstein distance between
x_{k+1}={\Phi}{(\delta,x_{k},{F^{k}},\beta,\mathbf{B}_{k}^{x},0)}
and
\bar{x}_{k+1}=\Exp_{x_{k}}\bigl(\delta\beta(x_{k})+\sqrt{\delta}\zeta_{k}\bigr)
, where
F^{k}
is an orthonormal basis of
x_{k}
and
\zeta_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}_{x_{k}}(0,I)
. We present our bound in Lemma
4
below:

Lemma 4
.

Let
\beta(\cdot)
be a vector field satisfying Assumption
2
. Consider arbitrary
x_{0}\in M
and let
E
be an orthonormal basis of
T_{x_{0}}M
. Let
\mathbf{B}
be a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
x^{0}(t)=\overline{\Phi}(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B},0)
and
x(t)=\Phi(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B})
as defined in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) respectively. (Existence of
x(t)
follows from Lemma
1
). Let
L_{1}
be any constant such that
L_{1}\geq L_{0}:={\left\|\beta(x_{0})\right\|}
and assume that
T\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{16{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}},\frac{1}{16L_{R}d},\frac{1}{16\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{1}}\right\}
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x^{0}(T),x(T))}}\right]\leq 2^{9}{(T^{2}L_{1}^{2}+T^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}+T^{3/2}{(d^{3/2}{(L_{R}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}/{L_{1}^{2}})}+L_{\beta}^{\prime}\sqrt{d})})}

Taking
T=\delta
, we show that the one-step discretization error is bounded by
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x_{k+1},\bar{x}_{k+1})}}\right]\lesssim O{(\delta^{3/2})}
. This
\delta^{3/2}
error scaling is the same as the error of Euler-Murayama in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

(
Durmus and Moulines 2017
)
. We are not aware of existing results bounding the error of Euler-Murayama on general manifolds. We defer the proof to Section
A.3
.

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

II. An SDE Construction.

The proof of Lemma
4
works by bounding distance between adjacent trajectories
x^{i}(t)
and
x^{i+1}(t)
defined in (
3.2
). The analysis crucially relies the on the fact that
x^{i}(t)
and
x^{i+1}(t)
are “almost” synchronously coupled—see discussion in Section
3.1
.

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

III. Contraction via Kendall-Cranston (Reflection Coupling).

The last part involves applying Lemma
3
to show that when
x(t)
and
y(t)
evolve under the exact SDE,
f(\mathrm{d}{(x(t),y(t))})
contracts with rate
\alpha
. We again highlight the fact that Lemma 3 is an existing result, see Remark
2
.

4
Diffusion Approximation of Non-Gaussian walk

We now study how to approximate the SDE (
1.1
) by a discrete random walk with non-Gaussian noise. Let
\beta
be a deterministic and
\xi
a random vector field
\xi
; given an initial point
y\in M
, we define a one-step walk with drift vector field
\beta
and noise
\xi
(in the form of a random vector field) as

\displaystyle\Psi(\delta;y,\beta,\xi):=\Exp_{y}\bigl(\delta\beta(y)+\sqrt{\delta}\xi(y)\bigr).

(4.1)

Next, we introduce key assumptions on the manifold and the noise. Our first assumption bounds the first and second derivatives of the Riemannian curvature tensor:

Assumption 5
.

We assume that the manifold
M
has Riemannian curvature tensor
R
satisfying

1.

For all
x\in M
,
u,v,w,z,a\in T_{x}M
,
\bm{\left\langle}(\nabla_{a}R)(u,v)w,z\bm{}\leq L_{R}^{\prime}{\left\|u\right\|}{\left\|v\right\|}{\left\|w\right\|}{\left\|z\right\|}{\left\|a\right\|}

2.

For all
x\in M
,
u,v,w,z,a,b\in T_{x}M
,
\bm{\left\langle}(\nabla_{b}(\nabla_{a}R))(u,v)w,z\bm{}\leq L_{R}^{\prime\prime}{\left\|u\right\|}{\left\|v\right\|}{\left\|w\right\|}{\left\|z\right\|}{\left\|a\right\|}{\left\|b\right\|}

Our second assumption bounds the magnitude of
\xi
and its derivatives up to second order:

Assumption 6

(Noise regularity)
.

The random vector field
\xi
satisfies, with probability 1,

(i)

{\left\|\xi(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\xi}
;

(ii)

{\left\|{(\nabla_{v_{1}}\xi)}(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\xi}^{\prime}
;

(iii)

{\left\|{(\nabla_{v_{2}}\nabla_{v_{1}}\xi)}(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\xi}^{\prime\prime}
.

for all
x
and for all
v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\in T_{x}M
with
{\left\|v_{1}\right\|}={\left\|v_{2}\right\|}={\left\|v_{3}\right\|}=1
.

Lastly, we assume that the noise
\xi
has
0
mean and identity covariance:

Assumption 7

(Moments of
\xi
)
.

For all
x\in M
, the random vector field
\xi
in satisfies:

\displaystyle 1.\ \mathbb{E}\left[{\xi(x)}\right]=0\qquad\qquad 2.\ \mathrm{Cov}(\xi(x))=I.

More precisely, let
E_{1},\ldots,E_{d}
be any orthonormal basis of
T_{x}M
. Let
{\bm{\xi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the coordinates of
\xi
wrt
E_{1},\ldots,E_{d}
. Then 2. is saying that
\mathbb{E}\left[{{\bm{\xi}}(x){\bm{\xi}}(x)^{T}}\right]=I
.

Given these assumptions, we are ready to state the second main result of this paper:

Theorem 2
.

Assume
M
satisfies Assumptions
3
,
4
and
5
. Let
\beta
be a vector field satisfying Assumption
2
. Assume in addition that there exists
L_{\beta}
such that for all
x\in M
,
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}
. Let
\xi
be a vector field that satisfies Assumption
6
and Assumption
7
. There exist a constant
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
, which depend polynomially on
L_{\beta},L_{\beta}^{\prime},L_{\xi},L_{\xi}^{\prime},L_{\xi}^{\prime\prime},L_{R},L_{R}^{\prime}
, such that for any positive
T\leq\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{1}}
, the following holds:

Let
\delta:={T}^{3}
and
K:=T/\delta
. Let
y_{0}
be arbitrary, let
x^{*}
be any point with
\beta(x^{*})=0
. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, let
y_{k+1}=\Psi(\delta;y_{k},\beta,\xi_{k})
, where
\Psi
is as defined (
4.1
), and let
\bar{y}_{k}:=\overline{\Phi}(k\delta;y_{0},E,\beta,\mathbf{B},0)
, where
\overline{\Phi}
is as defined in (
3.3
),
E
is an orthonormal basis at
T_{y_{0}}M
,
\mathbf{B}
is a standard Euclidean Brownian motion., there is a coupling between
y_{K}
and
\bar{y}_{K}
for which

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(\bar{y}_{K},y_{K})}}\right]\leq\tilde{O}{(T^{3/2}{(1+L_{\beta}^{2})})}

where
\tilde{O}
hides polynomial dependence on
L_{R},L_{R}^{\prime},L_{\xi},L_{\xi}^{\prime},L_{\xi}^{\prime\prime},\log{(\frac{1}{T})}
.

We defer the proof of Theorem
2
to Appendix
C.1
.

4.1
Discussion of Theorem
2

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

Short-time discretization bound
Theorem
2
bounds the distance between a discrete semimartingale with non-Gaussian increments (
y_{k}
), and an single geometric Euler Murayama step (
\bar{y}_{k}
). We assume that
\xi_{k}
, the
0
-mean noise in
y_{k}
, has bounded derivatives (Assumption
6
), and identity covariance (Assumption
7
), but can otherwise be arbitrary. Notably, the distribution of
\xi
, need
not
be rotationally invariant, and hence not invariant under parallel transport.

Theorem
2
bounds the distance between
y_{k}
and
\bar{y}_{k}
by
O{(T^{3/2})}
, and is thus meaningful only when
T=K\delta
is small. Thus Theorem
2
is most analogous to Lemma
4
, which shows that the distance between the exact SDE in (
1.1
) and its Euler-Murayama discretization is also bounded by
O(T^{3/2})
.

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

Long-time behavior and distance to exact SDE.
Similar to how Theorem
1
applies Lemma
4
recursively to show that the Euler-Murayama discretization converges to the exact SDE over a long period of time, we can also apply Theorem
2
+Lemma
4
recursively to show that
y_{k}
converges to the same SDE over a long period of time. Our result is summarized in Lemma
5
below. According to Lemma
5
, for the non-Gaussian random semimartingale
y_{NK}
to be
\varepsilon
-close to the exact SDE
x_{NK}
in Wasserstein distance,
\delta
needs to be order of
\varepsilon^{6}
. The first term requires number of steps
NK=\tilde{O}{(\log(1/\varepsilon)/\delta)}=\tilde{O}{(\varepsilon^{-6})}

Lemma 5
.

Assume
M,\beta,\xi
satisfy the same assumptions as Theorem
2
. Further let
\beta
satisfy Assumption
1
with
m,q,\mathcal{R}
, such that
m\geq 2L_{\Ric}
, and
q+L_{\Ric}\geq 0
. Let
\alpha
be as in Theorem
1
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Let
\delta:={T}^{3}
and
K:=T/\delta
. Let
x^{*}
be any point with
\beta(x^{*})=0
. For
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, let
y_{k+1}=\Psi(\delta;y_{k},\beta,\xi_{k})
,where
\Psi
is as defined (
4.1
). For
i\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, let
x_{k+1}:=\Phi(K\delta;x_{iK},{F^{iK}},\beta,\mathbf{B}^{iK})
, where
\Psi
is as defined in (
4.1
) and
\Phi
is defined by (
3.4
),
F^{iK}
is an orthonormal basis at
T_{x_{iK}}M
, and
\mathbf{B}^{iK}
are independent Brownian motions. Assume that
\mathrm{d}{(x_{0},x^{*})}\leq 2\mathcal{R}
and
\mathrm{d}{(y_{0},x^{*})}\leq 2\mathcal{R}
.

For any
N
, there exist a constant
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
, which depends polynomially on
L_{\beta},L_{\beta}^{\prime},L_{\xi},L_{\xi}^{\prime},L_{\xi}^{\prime\prime},L_{R},L_{R}^{\prime}
,

d,\mathcal{R},\log N
, such that if
T\leq\frac{1}{{\mathcal{C}}_{2}}
, then there is a coupling between
x_{NK}
and
y_{NK}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(x_{NK},{y}_{NK})}}\right]\leq\exp{(-\alpha NK\delta+{(q+L_{Ric})}\mathcal{R}^{2})}\mathrm{d}{(x_{0},y_{0})}+\exp{({(q+L_{Ric})}\mathcal{R}^{2})}\cdot\tilde{O}{(\delta^{1/6})},

where
\tilde{O}
hides polynomial dependency on
L_{\beta},L_{\beta}^{\prime},L_{\xi},L_{\xi}^{\prime},L_{\xi}^{\prime\prime},L_{R},L_{R}^{\prime},L_{R}^{\prime\prime},\frac{1}{m},d,\mathcal{R},\log{N},\log\frac{1}{\delta}
.

We defer the proof of Lemma
5
to Appendix
C.1
.

4.2
Proof Sketch and Theoretical Contributions

Below, we sketch the proof of Theorem
2
.
To simplify presentation, we will assume that
\beta=0
in this sketch. This still captures the essence of the proof as the main bottleneck in the bound is due to the noise terms
\xi_{k}
.

1.

Define
\bar{y}_{K}:=\Exp_{y_{0}}{(\sqrt{K\delta}\zeta)}
where
\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}_{y_{0}}{(0,I)}
, i.e.
\bar{y}_{K}
is a single Euler Murayama step, of stepsize
K\delta
. We show that, conditioned on
\xi_{0},\ldots,\xi_{K-1}
, there is some coupling between
\zeta
and
\xi_{0},\ldots,\xi_{K-1}
, such that

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y_{K},\bar{y}_{K})}}\right]=\tilde{O}{({(K\delta)}^{3/2})}
.

2.

We now bound the distance between
\bar{y}_{K}
and the exact SDE
\hat{y}_{K}:=\Phi(K\delta;y_{0},{E},\hat{\mathbf{B}}^{i})
. By Lemma
4
,
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(\bar{y}_{K},\hat{y}_{K})}}\right]\leq\tilde{O}{({(K\delta)}^{3/2})}
.

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

I. A random walk on
T_{y_{0}}M
.

Our first step is to construct a discrete stochastic process
\tilde{z}_{k}
in
T_{y_{0}}M
, such that
\tilde{y}_{k}:=\Exp_{z_{k}}
approximates
y_{k}
for
k=0...K
. This corresponds to Step 1 in the proof of Theorem
2
in Section
C.1
. Specifically,
\tilde{z}_{k}
and
\tilde{y}_{k}
are defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{y}_{0}=y_{0},\qquad\tilde{z}_{0}=0,

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(t)=\Exp_{\tilde{y}_{0}}{((1-t)\tilde{z}_{k})},\quad\tilde{z}_{k+1}=\tilde{z}_{k}+\sqrt{\delta}\Gamma_{\gamma_{k}(t)}{(\xi_{k}(\tilde{y}_{k}))},\quad\tilde{y}_{k+1}=\Exp_{y_{0}}{(\tilde{z}_{k+1})}.

(4.2)

Our goal in step I is to show the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{y}_{K},y_{K})}}\right]\leq\tilde{O}{({(K\delta)}^{3/2})}=\tilde{O}{({(T)}^{3/2})},

(4.3)

The key to proving the above inequality is Lemma
78
: given
x\in M
and
u\in T_{x}M
, let
x^{\prime}:=\Exp_{x}(u)
, then for any
v\in T_{x}M
, there exists a tensor
G
, that depends only on
x
, such that

\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{x}(u+v),\Exp_{x^{\prime}}(\Gamma_{x}^{x^{\prime}}G(v)))}\leq O{({\left\|u\right\|}^{2}{\left\|v\right\|}^{4})}.

Furthermore,
{\left\|G-Id\right\|}=O{({\left\|u\right\|}^{2})}
.

Consider a fixed step
k
, and let
x=y_{0},x^{\prime}=\tilde{y}_{k},u=\tilde{z}_{k},v=\Gamma_{\tilde{y}_{k}}^{y_{0}}{(\sqrt{\delta}\xi_{k}{(\tilde{y}_{k})})}
. Notice that
\tilde{z}_{k}
is the sum of
k
random variables each of variance scaled by
\delta
. Thus with high probability,
{\left\|u\right\|}^{2}\leq O{(K\delta)}
and
{\left\|v\right\|}^{2}\leq O{(\delta)}
, so that

O{({\left\|u\right\|}^{2}{\left\|v\right\|}^{4})}=O{(K\delta^{3})}
. Lemma
78
thus guarantees that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{y}_{k+1},\Exp_{\tilde{y}_{k}}{(\sqrt{\delta}G(\xi(\tilde{y}_{k})))})}^{2}\leq O{(K\delta^{3})}

(4.4)

On the other hand, we can use a basic "synchronous-coupling-like" analysis (Lemma
63
) to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y_{k+1},\Exp_{\tilde{y}_{k}}{(\sqrt{\delta}G(\xi(\tilde{y}_{k})))})}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle{(1+\delta)}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y_{k},\tilde{y}_{k})}^{2}}\right]-2\sqrt{\delta}\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{\left\langle}\Exp_{\tilde{y}_{k}}^{-1},G(\xi_{k}(\tilde{y}_{k}))-\Gamma_{y_{k}}^{\tilde{y}_{k}}\xi_{k}(y_{k})\bm{}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\delta\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|G(\xi_{k}(\tilde{y}_{k}))-\Gamma_{y_{k}}^{\tilde{y}_{k}}\xi_{k}(y_{k})\right\|}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{(1+\delta)}\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(y_{k},\tilde{y}_{k})}^{2}}\right]+K^{2}\delta^{3}

(4.5)

Note that the above is simplified and keeps only the
K
and
\delta
dependence. The last line uses the facts that
1.\mathbb{E}\left[{\xi_{k}(x)}\right]=0
,
2.G(v)
is linear in
v
, and
3.{\left\|G-Id\right\|}^{2}\leq O{({\left\|u\right\|}^{4})}=O{(K^{2}\delta^{2})}
. Combining (
4.4
) and (
4.5
), and recursing over
k=K...0
gives (
4.3
). Detailed steps can be found in the proof of Corollary
15
.

Remark 3
.

Lemma
78
is similar to Lemma 3 of
Sun et al. 2019
, which we restate as Lemma
13
in the appendix. Roughly speaking, this Lemma provides a bound of the form

\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{x}(u+v),\Exp_{x^{\prime}}(\Gamma_{x}^{x^{\prime}}G(v)))}\leq O{({\left\|u\right\|}^{2}{\left\|v\right\|}^{2}{({\left\|u\right\|}^{2}+{\left\|v\right\|}^{2})})}
, which ends up being
O{(K^{2}\delta^{3})}
. Compared with (
4.4
), we see that there is an additional factor of
K
, which would eventually lead to a bound of
O{(K^{2}\delta^{3/2})}
instead of (
4.3
). The essential difference is our bound is that we pull out the linear error term
G()
separately; later on, when bounding (
4.5
), the cross term involving
G(\xi(\cdot))
has
0
expectation due to linearity, so that it does not pose an issue. We believe that Lemma
78
may be of independent interest when one needs to analyze retractions of semimartingales in other settings.

\color[rgb]{0.55,0.55,0.55}{\blacksquare}

II. A variant of Central Limit Theorem.

Our goal in this step is to bound
W_{2}{(\tilde{z}_{K},\sqrt{K\delta}\zeta)}
, where
\zeta\sim\mathcal{N}_{y_{0}}(0,I)
. Observe that
\tilde{z}_{K}
is the sum of
\sqrt{\delta}\Gamma_{\tilde{y}_{k}}^{y_{0}}\xi_{k}(\tilde{y}_{k})
for
k=0,\ldots,K-1
. We assumed that
\xi_{k}(x)
has
0
-mean and identity covariance for all
x
. Lemma
67
implies that the parallel transport of
\xi_{k}
must also have
0
-mean and identity covariance. If the
\xi_{k}
’s are independent, then standard quantitative CLT (e.g.
Zhai 2018
) immediately imply that
\tilde{z}_{K}
is close to a
\mathcal{N}(0,K\delta I)
. However, each
\xi_{k}(\tilde{y}_{k})
depends on
\xi_{0}(\tilde{y_{0}}),\ldots,\xi_{k-1}(\tilde{y}_{k-1})
, because it depends on
\tilde{y}_{k}
. In Lemma
42
, we show that the CLT approximation still holds if 1.
\xi_{k}
’s have the correct covariance, and 2.
\xi_{k}
’s have bounded derivatives, up to second order. Quantitatively, Lemma
42
guarantees that

\displaystyle W_{2}{(\tilde{z}_{K},\sqrt{K\delta}\zeta)}\leq\tilde{O}{(\delta^{1/2})}=\tilde{O}{(T^{3/2})}

(4.6)

where we use the definition of
\delta=(K\delta)^{3}=T^{3}
.

Theorem
2
follows almost immediately from two observations: First,
y_{K}=\overline{\Phi}(K\delta;y_{0},E,\beta,\mathbf{B},0)
is equal in distribution to
\Exp_{y_{0}}{(\sqrt{K\delta}\zeta)}
. Second,
\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{y}_{K},\bar{y}_{K})}=\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{y_{0}}{(\tilde{z}_{K})},\Exp_{y_{0}}(\sqrt{K\delta}\zeta))}\lesssim{\left\|\tilde{z}_{K},\sqrt{K\delta}\zeta\right\|}
, where we use Lemma
63
in the last step. Theorem
2
follows immediately from summing (
4.3
) and (
4.6
).

5
Stochastic Gradient Descent and Generalization Error

In this section, we show how our analysis of the diffusion on Riemannian manifold can lead to a generalization bound for SGD. We begin with setting up the notations and explain why SGD can be viewed as a Riemannian diffusion process.

5.1
Setup

To distinguish from
\nabla
(Levi Civita connection), we use the bold version
{\bm{\nabla}}
to denote the
Euclidean derivative
of a function
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, i.e.
\frac{d}{dt}h(x+tv)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}{({\bm{\nabla}}h(x))}_{i}\cdot v_{i}
.

Let
\ell(x,s):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{S}\to\mathbb{R}
denote a loss function, with
x
being model parameters (e.g. neural network weights) and
s
being a sample (e.g. vector of pixel values). Let
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{n-1},s_{n}\}
denote a set of
n
i.i.d samples. Let
\ell_{n}(x):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\ell(x,s)
be the expected gradient wrt uniform distribution over the training set. We are interested in the following sequence:

\displaystyle x_{k+1}=x_{k}-\delta{\bm{\nabla}}\ell_{n}(x_{k})+\sqrt{\delta}\xi_{k}(x_{k}),

(5.1)

where
\xi_{k}
are i.i.d random vector fields satisfying
\mathbb{E}\left[{\xi_{k}(x)}\right]=0
and

\mathbb{E}\left[{\xi_{k}(x)\xi_{k}(x)^{T}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{{({\bm{\nabla}}\ell(x,s)-{\bm{\nabla}}\ell_{n}(x))}{({\bm{\nabla}}\ell(x,s)-{\bm{\nabla}}\ell_{n}(x))}^{T}}\right]
. Let us denote
A_{n}(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[{\xi_{k}(x)\xi_{k}(x)^{T}}\right]
, where expectation is wrt
s\sim Uniform(\mathcal{S}_{n})
. Let us also define the following Euclidean SDE, initialized at
y(0)=x_{0}
:

\displaystyle dy(t)=-{\bm{\nabla}}\ell_{n}(y(t))dt+A_{n}(y(t))^{1/2}d\mathbf{W}(t)

(5.2)

where
\mathbf{W}(t)
is a Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We will see in Section
5.3
that
x_{k}
in (
5.1
) approximates (
5.2
). Finally, let us define the Riemannian manifold
(\mathbb{R}^{d},g)
, where
g
is the metric tensor, given by
g(x)=A_{n}(x)^{-1}
, where
A_{n}(x)
is the empirical covariance of gradient at
x
defined earlier.

Notice that the noise scaling is
\sqrt{\delta}
, whereas in standard SGD, the
0
-mean noise term has scaling
\delta
. In the study of SGD noise and generalization error, various authors
Hoffer et al. 2017
;
Cheng et al. 2020
;
Li et al. 2021
have proposed the injection of additional noise into SGD iterates, so that the second moment of the noise term is constant even as stepsize goes to
0
. In a related vein, the "linear-scaling-rule", which suggests to multiply stepsize by
c
whenever batchsize is multiplied by
c
, also works to effectively maintain a constant ratio for
\frac{{\left\|A_{n}\right\|}_{2}}{\delta}
. (
Krizhevsky et al. 2009
;
Goyal et al. 2017
, see also the discussion in
Li et al. 2021
) Our motivation here is to analyze these "noisy SGD" variants. We do not specify an explicit form for
\xi
, but only impose various regularity assumptions such as (
6
). This is so our analysis applies for different noise injection schemes, such as adding the difference of indpendently sampled gradients
Cheng et al. 2020
;
Li et al. 2021
, or multiplying the stochastic gradient by a Gaussian noise
Hoffer et al. 2017
.

5.2
The Generalization Gap from Riemannian Diffusion

We study the generalization performance of SGD via Riemannian diffusion following the stability approach in
Bousquet and Elisseeff 2002
;
Hardt et al. 2016
. Recall the definitions of
\mathcal{S}_{n}=\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{n-1},s_{n}\}
,
\ell_{n}
,
g_{n}
,
A_{n}
from the previous sections. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n}:=\{s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2},s_{n-1},s_{n}\}
be an adjacent set of samples, where
s_{1}^{\prime}
is independently sampled, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}_{n}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{n}
differ only in the first sample.
Let
\tilde{\ell}_{n}
denote the expected empirical loss on
\mathcal{S}_{n}
, and let
\tilde{g}_{n}(x)
denote the empirical covariance of
{\bm{\nabla}}\ell(x,\cdot)
. Let
\tilde{A}_{n}(x):=\tilde{g}_{n}(x)^{-1}
. Let
\tilde{\xi}_{k}
be i.i.d random vector fields with
0
-mean and covariance
\tilde{A}_{n}(x)
.

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{k+1}=\tilde{x}_{k}-\delta{\bm{\nabla}}\tilde{\ell}_{n}(\tilde{x}_{k})+\sqrt{\delta}\tilde{\xi}_{k}(\tilde{x}_{k}),

(5.3)

Theorem 3

(Algorithm stability, informal)
.

Denote
x_{t},x_{t}^{\prime}
as the iterates generated by equation
5.1
and
5.3
with data set
\mathcal{S}_{n}
and
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
respectively. Then for positive integers
K=\mathcal{O}(n^{4}),\delta=\mathcal{O}(n^{-6})
, we have that for
t=K,2K,3K...
,

\displaystyle W_{1}(x_{t},x_{t}^{\prime})\leq\min\{\mathcal{O}(t\delta n^{-1}),\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})\},

where the hidden constants depend on constants in assumptions but are independent of
n,t,\delta,K
.

Detailed theorem statement, along with the assumptions needed, can be found in Theorem
4
in Appendix
J.6
. We discuss Theorem
3
in more detail at the end in Section
5.3
. Before that, we state the following generalization guarantee on SGD:

Lemma 6

(Generalization bound)
.

Denote
x_{t},
as the iterates generated by equation
5.1
with data set
\mathcal{S}_{n}
. Then for positive integers
K=\mathcal{O}(n^{4}),\delta=\mathcal{O}(n^{-6})
, we have that for
t=K,2K,3K...
, the generalization gap satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{S,s^{\prime}\mathcal{A}}\bigl[f(s^{\prime},x_{t})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(s_{i},x_{t})\bigr]\leq\min\{\mathcal{O}(t\delta n^{-1}),\mathcal{O}(n^{-1})\},

where
s^{\prime}
denotes an independent test sample. The hidden constants depend on constants in assumptions but are independent of
n,t,\delta,K
.

5.3
Discussion and Proof Outline of Theorem
4

There are numerous assumptions used in Theorem
4
. They fall into three categories:

I

That
\lambda_{A}I\prec A(x)\prec L_{A}I
(Assumption
8
). This essentially ensures that, up to constants, the Riemannian metric is equivalent to the Euclidean metric. This assumption is satisfied if the covariance matrix of
\xi
is lower and upper bounded.

II

That the vector field
{\bm{\nabla}}\ell_{n}(x)+\phi(x)
(
\phi
being the trace of the Christoffel symbol defined in (
J.3
))
satisfies the
distant-dissipativity
assumption (Assumption
1
). This is required to apply Lemma
3
in order to show that (
5.2
) mixes in Riemannian distance.

III

Various smoothness assumptions, such as
{\left\|{\bm{\nabla}}\ell(x,s)-{\bm{\nabla}}\ell(y,s)\right\|}_{2}\leq L_{\ell}^{\prime}{\left\|x-y\right\|}_{2}
,
{\left\|\xi(x)-\xi(y)\right\|}_{2}\leq L_{\xi}^{\prime}{\left\|x-y\right\|}_{2}
(Assumption
11
) and
\text{tr}{(A_{n}(x)-A_{n}(y))}^{2}\leq{L_{A}^{\prime}}^{2}{\left\|x-y\right\|}_{2}^{2}
(Assumption
9
). These assumptions are satisfied by assuming bounded derivatives of sufficient order on
\ell
and
\xi
.

The proof of Theorem
4
can be decomposed as follows: First, in Lemma
83
, we show that
x_{k}
(as defined in (
5.1
)) can be approximated by the sequence
z_{k+1}=\Exp_{z_{k}}{(-\delta{\bm{\nabla}}\ell_{n}(x_{k})+\sqrt{\delta}\xi_{k}(x_{k})+\frac{\delta}{2}\phi(z_{k}))}
, with error
\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|x_{K}-z_{K}\right\|}_{2}^{2}}\right]=O{(K\delta^{2})}
. The
\phi
is a second-order correction term arising due to interaction between
\sqrt{\delta}\xi
and the Riemannian curvature. Together with Theorem
2
and Lemma
84
, we show in Lemma
85
that
\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|x_{K}-y(K\delta)\right\|}_{2}}\right]\leq K\delta\cdot\delta^{1/6}
, where
y(t)
is as defined in (
5.2
).

We highlight that Lemma
83
may be of independent interest. For example, the discrete walk in Theorem
1
may not be easily computable on a general manifold. Often, the algorithm has to work in some Euclidean coordinate system, and Lemma
83
allows us to approximate one step of (
1.3
) by a step in Euclidean coordinates.

We repeat Step 1 to show that
\tilde{x}_{k+1}
in (
5.3
) is similarly approximated by
d\tilde{y}(t)=-{\bm{\nabla}}\tilde{\ell}_{n}(\tilde{y}(t))dt+\tilde{A}_{n}(\tilde{y}(t))^{1/2}d\mathbf{W}(t)
. We then define
d\bar{y}(t)=-{\bm{\nabla}}\ell_{n}(\bar{y}(t))dt+{A}_{n}(\tilde{y}(t))^{1/2}d\mathbf{W}(t)
. We show in Lemma
87
that
\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|\tilde{y}(t)-\bar{y}(t)\right\|}_{2}^{2}}\right]=O{(t/(n^{2}))}
. We use Lemma
84
to verify that
y
and
\bar{y}
are equivalent to SDEs on the manifold
(\mathbb{R}^{d},g)
. Lemma
3
the implies that
\mathrm{d}{(y(K\delta),\bar{y}(K\delta))}
contracts in terms of a Lyapunov function (this step crucially relies on II).

Finally, we remark that as an alternative, we can prove the contraction of
y(K\delta)-\bar{y}(K\delta)
, under assumptions of Euclidean distant-dissipativity instead (see, e.g. Assumption 3.2 of
Gorham et al. 2019
). Euclidean distant dissipativity is easier to verify, since it is guaranteed by adding a sufficiently strongly convex regularizer outside a ball, but may lead to worse mixing rate. We remark that our stability bound in Theorem
2
is unchanged in the big-O sense if we use this approach.

Appendix A
Manifold SDE

In this section, we state and prove key Lemmas related to our construction in Section
3.1
.

We prove the existence of limit in Lemma
1
, the equivalence to manifold SDE in Lemma
2
. We also state our key discretization error bound for Euler Murayama discretization in Lemma
4
. Finally, we provide the proof of Theorem
1
.

A.1
Existence

Lemma 7
.

Let
T
be any positive constant. Let
x^{i}(t)
be as defined in (
3.3
).

Assume that there is are constants
L_{\beta},L_{\beta}^{\prime}
such that for all
x,y\in M
,
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}
and

{\left\|\beta(x)-\Gamma_{y}^{x}\beta(y)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}^{\prime}{\left\|x-y\right\|}
.

Then there exists a constant
{\mathcal{C}}
, which depends on
L_{R},T,d
, such that for all

,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}-2}})}

\displaystyle\leq e^{{(2^{6-i}T{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{2}+2L_{R}d+L_{\beta}^{\prime})}T}T^{2}\cdot{({\delta^{i}}^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{i}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-i/2+14}

Proof.

Let us define

\displaystyle a_{k}:=\delta^{i+1}\beta{(x^{i+1}_{2k})}+{{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+1)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{(2k\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ E^{i+1}_{2k}

\displaystyle b_{k}:=\delta^{i+1}\beta{(x^{i+1}_{2k})}+{{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+2)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{({(2k+1)}\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ E^{i+1}_{2k}

(A.1)

Our proof breaks down the bound of
\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x^{i+1}_{2k+2})}
into two parts: by Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k+1},x^{i+1}_{2k+2})}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle{(\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k+1},\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k}+b_{k})})}+\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k}+b_{k})},x^{i+1}_{2k+2})})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{(1+\frac{1}{2K})}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k+1},\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k}+b_{k})})}^{2}+K\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k}+b_{k})},x^{i+1}_{2k+2})}^{2}

(A.2)

We now bound the first term of (
A.2
). From definition in (
3.1
) and (
A.1
),

\displaystyle x^{i}_{k+1}=

\displaystyle\Exp_{x^{i}_{k}}{(\delta^{i}\beta{(x^{i}_{k})}+{{(\mathbf{B}{({(k+1)}\delta^{i})}-\mathbf{B}{(k\delta^{i})})}}\circ E^{i}_{k})}

\displaystyle\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k}+b_{k})}=

\displaystyle\Exp_{x^{i}_{k}}{(\delta^{i}\beta{(x^{i+1}_{2k})}+{{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+2)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{(2k\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ E^{i+1}_{2k})}

We thus apply Lemma
63
, with
x:=x^{i}_{k}
,
y:=x^{i+1}_{2k}
,
u:=\delta^{i}\beta{(x^{i}_{k})}+{{(\mathbf{B}{({(k+1)}\delta^{i})}-\mathbf{B}{(k\delta^{i})})}}\circ E^{i}_{k}
,
v:=\delta^{i}\beta{(x^{i+1}_{2k})}+{{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+2)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{(2k\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ E^{i+1}_{k}
. Let
\gamma(t),u(t),v(t)
be as defined in Lemma

. Then Lemma

bounds

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{x}(u),\Exp_{y}(v))}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle{(1+4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{2}e^{4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}})}\mathrm{d}{(x,y)}^{2}+32e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}{\left\|v(0)-u(0)\right\|}^{2}+2\bm{\left\langle}\gamma^{\prime}(0),v(0)-u(0)\bm{}

(A.3)

where

.

Some of the terms above can be simplified. We begin by bounding the

\beta
is Lipschitz,
{\left\|\delta^{i}\beta(x^{i}_{k})-\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i}_{k}}\delta^{i}\beta(x^{i+1}_{2k})\right\|}\leq\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x^{i+1}_{2k})}
. By definition of
E^{i+1}_{2k}
from (
3.1
),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i}_{k}}{({{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+2)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{(2k\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ E^{i+1}_{2k})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+2)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{(2k\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ{(\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i}_{k}}E^{i+1}_{2k})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{{(\mathbf{B}{({(k+1)}\delta^{i})}-\mathbf{B}{(k\delta^{i})})}}\circ E^{i}_{k}

where the last line is because
\delta^{i}=2\delta^{i+1}
and because
E^{i+1}_{2k}:=\Gamma_{x^{i}_{k}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(E^{i}_{k})}
from (
3.1
).

Thus

\displaystyle{{(\mathbf{B}{({(k+1)}\delta^{i})}-\mathbf{B}{(k\delta^{i})})}}\circ E^{i}_{k}-\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i}_{k}}{({{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+2)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{(2k\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ E^{i+1}_{2k})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{{(\mathbf{B}{({(k+1)}\delta^{i})}-\mathbf{B}{(k\delta^{i})})}}\circ E^{i}_{k}-{{(\mathbf{B}{({(k+1)}\delta^{i})}-\mathbf{B}{(k\delta^{i})})}}\circ\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i}_{k}}E^{i}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

We can thus bound via Young’s Inequality:

\displaystyle{\left\|u(0)-v(0)\right\|}^{2}\leq 2{\delta^{i}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x^{i+1}_{2k})}^{2}

Finally,

\displaystyle 2\bm{\left\langle}\gamma^{\prime}(0),v(0)-u(0)\bm{}=2\bm{\left\langle}\Exp_{x^{i}_{k}}^{-1}{(x^{i+1}_{2k})},\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i}_{k}}\delta^{i}\beta(x^{i+1}_{2k})-\delta^{i}\beta(x^{i}_{k})\bm{}\leq 2\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x^{i+1}_{2k})}^{2}

Plugging into
A.3

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k+1},\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k}+b_{k})})}^{2}\leq{(1+4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{2}e^{4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}+64e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x^{i+1}_{2k})}^{2}

We now bound the second term of (
A.2
). Let us introduce two more convenient definitions:

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{k}:=\delta^{i}\beta{(x^{i+1}_{2k+1})}+{{(\mathbf{B}{({(2k+2)}\delta^{i+1})}-\mathbf{B}{({(2k+1)}\delta^{i+1})})}}\circ E^{i+1}_{2k+1}

\displaystyle z:=\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k})}

It follows from definition that

\displaystyle x^{i+1}_{2k+2}=\Exp_{z}{(b^{\prime}_{k})}

We break the bound on

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k}+b_{k})},x^{i+1}_{2k+2})}\leq

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k}+b_{k})},\Exp_{z}{(\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k+1}}b_{k})})}+\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{z}{(\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k+1}}b_{k})},\Exp_{z}{(b^{\prime}_{k})})}

To bound the first term, we apply Lemma
13
with

,
a=a_{k}
,

, to get

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}{(a_{k}+b_{k})},\Exp_{z}{(\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k+1}}b_{k})})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L_{R}{\left\|a_{k}\right\|}{\left\|b_{k}\right\|}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|})}e^{\sqrt{L_{R}}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|})}}

To bound the second term, we apply Lemma

(with

), so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(\Exp_{z}{(\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k+1}}b_{k})},\Exp_{z}{(b^{\prime}_{k})})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 32e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{\prime}}{\left\|\Gamma_{x^{i+1}_{2k}}^{x^{i+1}_{2k+1}}b_{k}-b^{\prime}_{k}\right\|}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 64e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{\prime}}{\delta^{i+1}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i+1}_{2k},x^{i+1}_{2k+1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 128e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{\prime}}{\delta^{i+1}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{\left\|a_{k}\right\|}_{2}^{2}

where we define

.

Plugging everything into (
A.2
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k+1},x^{i+1}_{2k+2})}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle{(1+\frac{1}{2K})}{(1+4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{2}e^{4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}+64e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x^{i+1}_{2k})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+32KL_{R}^{2}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{6}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|}^{6})}e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|})}}+256Ke^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{\prime}}{\delta^{i+1}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{2}

In fact, if we consider any

, and using the definition of

, we can extend the bound to

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[k\delta^{i},(k+1)\delta^{i})}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{(1+\frac{1}{2K})}{(1+4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{2}e^{4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}+64e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime})}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k},x^{i+1}_{2k})}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+32KL_{R}^{2}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{6}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|}^{6})}e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|})}}+256Ke^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{\prime}}{\delta^{i+1}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{2}

(A.4)

Let us define

\displaystyle r_{0}=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle r_{k+1}^{2}:=

\displaystyle{(1+\frac{1}{2K})}{(1+4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{2}e^{4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}+64e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime})}r_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+32KL_{R}^{2}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{6}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|}^{6})}e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|})}}+256Ke^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{\prime}}{\delta^{i+1}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{2}

It follows from (
A.4
) that
r_{k}\geq\sup_{t\in[(k-1)\delta^{i},k\delta^{i})}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}
and that
r_{k+1}\geq r_{k}
with probability 1, for all
k
, so that
\sup_{t\leq T}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\leq r_{K}
. We will now bound
\mathbb{E}\left[{r_{K}^{2}}\right]
, and then apply Markov’s Inequality. Let us define
{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
to be the
\sigma
-field generated by
\mathbf{B}(t)
for
t\in[0,k\delta^{i})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}}\left[{r_{k+1}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}}\left[{{(1+\frac{1}{2K})}{(1+4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{2}e^{4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}+64e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime})}}\right]r_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}}\left[{32KL_{R}^{2}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{6}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|}^{6})}e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|})}}+256Ke^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{\prime}}{\delta^{i+1}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{2}}\right]

We will bound the terms above one by one. First, note from definition that

{\mathcal{C}}_{k}\leq\sqrt{L_{R}}{(2\delta^{i}L_{\beta}+2{\left\|\mathbf{B}((k+1)\delta^{i})-\mathbf{B}(k\delta^{i})\right\|}_{2})}
. Let
\eta^{i}_{k}:=\mathbf{B}((k+1)\delta^{i})-\mathbf{B}(k\delta^{i})
.

For sufficiently large
i
,
\delta^{i}\leq{\sqrt{L_{R}}L_{\beta}/8}
. Simplifying,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}}\left[{{(1+\frac{1}{2K})}{(1+4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{2}e^{4{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}+64e^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}}\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime})}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{2K}+16{\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+16L_{R}\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}^{2}}\right]+16{\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[{e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}}}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{8L_{R}}{\delta^{i}d}\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}^{4}}\right]+8L_{R}\delta^{i}d\mathbb{E}\left[{e^{4\sqrt{L_{R}}{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}}}\right]+128\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}\left[{e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{2K}+16L_{R}{({\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}^{2}}\right]+\frac{1}{\delta^{i}d}\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}^{4}}\right])}+8\delta^{i}{(\delta^{i}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+L_{R}d+16L_{\beta}^{\prime})}\mathbb{E}\left[{e^{4\sqrt{L_{R}}{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{2K}+40{({\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+2\delta^{i}L_{R}d+\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime})}

where we use

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}^{2}}\right]=\delta^{i}d

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}^{2}}\right]\leq 2{\delta^{i}}^{2}d^{2}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{e^{4\sqrt{L_{R}}{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}}}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[{e^{8L_{R}{\left\|\eta^{i}_{k}\right\|}^{2}}}\right]\leq 2e^{16L_{R}\delta^{i}d}\leq 4

where we use Lemma
37
, and the fact that
\delta^{i}\leq\frac{1}{32L_{R}d}
for sufficiently large
i
.

Next, we bound
\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}}\left[{32KL_{R}^{2}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{6}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|}^{6})}e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|})}}}\right]
. Note that
{\left\|a_{k}\right\|}\leq\frac{\delta^{i}}{2}L_{\beta}+{\left\|\eta^{i+1}_{2k}\right\|}
and
{\left\|b_{k}\right\|}\leq\frac{\delta^{i}}{2}L_{\beta}+{\left\|\eta^{i+1}_{2k+1}\right\|}
. By similar argument as above,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}}\left[{32KL_{R}^{2}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{6}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|}^{6})}e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}{({\left\|a_{k}\right\|}+{\left\|b_{k}\right\|})}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2048KL_{R}^{2}e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}\delta^{i}L_{\beta}}\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}}\left[{{(2^{-5}{\delta^{i}}^{6}L_{\beta}^{6}+{\left\|\eta^{i+1}_{2k}\right\|}^{6}+{\left\|\eta^{i+1}_{2k}\right\|}^{6})}\cdot e^{2\sqrt{L_{R}}{({\left\|\eta^{i+1}_{2k}\right\|}+{\left\|\eta^{i+1}_{2k+1}\right\|})}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle KL_{R}^{2}{(512{\delta^{i}}^{6}L_{\beta}^{6}+2048{\delta^{i}}^{3}d^{3})}

Finally, note that
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{\prime}\leq 2\sqrt{L_{R}}{(L_{\beta}+{\left\|\eta^{i+1}_{2k+1}\right\|})}
, so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}}\left[{256Ke^{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}^{\prime}}{\delta^{i+1}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{\left\|a_{k}\right\|}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle 256K{\delta^{i}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{({\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+\delta^{i}d)}

Put together,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k}}}\left[{r_{k+1}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{(1+\frac{1}{2K}+40{({\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+2\delta^{i}L_{R}d+\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime})})}{(KL_{R}^{2}{(512{\delta^{i}}^{6}L_{\beta}^{6}+2048{\delta^{i}}^{3}d^{3})}+256K{\delta^{i}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}{({\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+\delta^{i}d)})}

(A.5)

Applying the above recursively and simplifying,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{r_{K}^{2}}\right]\leq e^{40K{\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+2K\delta^{i}L_{R}d+K\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime}}{(K{\delta^{i}})}^{2}{({\delta^{i}}^{4}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+{\delta^{i}}L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{i}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{\delta^{i}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{10}

(A.6)

By Markov’s Inequality, and recalling that
r_{k}
is w.p. 1 non-decreasing and

\sup_{t\leq T}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\leq R_{K}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}^{2}\geq 2^{-i/2-4}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{r_{K}^{2}}\right]\cdot 2^{i/2+4}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{{(40\delta^{i}{L_{R}}L_{\beta}^{2}+2L_{R}d+L_{\beta}^{\prime})}T}T^{2}\cdot{({\delta^{i}}^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{i}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-i/2+14}

(A.7)

∎

Proof of Lemma
1
.

Let us define
L_{0}:={\left\|\beta(x(0))\right\|}
.

Step 1: Bounding the probability of deviation between
x^{i}
and
x^{i+1}

We would like to apply Lemma
7
. However, note that Lemma
7
assumes that
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}
globally, which we do not assume here. We must therefore approximate
\beta
by a sequence of Lipschitz vector fields.

Let us define

\displaystyle\beta^{j}(x):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\beta(x)&\text{for }{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq 2^{j/2}\\
\beta(x)\cdot\frac{2^{j/2}}{{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}}&\text{for }{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}>2^{j/2}\end{array}\right.

Let us denote by
L_{\beta^{j}}:=2^{j/2}
. We verify that for all
x,y\in M
,
{\left\|\beta^{j}(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta^{j}}
and

{\left\|\beta^{j}(x)-\Gamma_{y}^{x}\beta^{j}(y)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}^{\prime}{\left\|x-y\right\|}
.

Finally, for any let
\tilde{x}^{i,j}(t)
be as defined in (
3.3
), with
\beta
replaced by
\beta^{j}
. Lemma
7
immediately implies that, for all
i\geq{\mathcal{C}}
(where
{\mathcal{C}}
is some constant depending on
L_{R},T,d
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}-2}})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq e^{{(40T{L_{R}}+2L_{R}d+L_{\beta}^{\prime})}T}T^{2}\cdot{(T^{3}L_{R}^{2}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+T{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-i/2+14}

where we use the fact that
\delta^{i}{L_{\beta}^{i}}^{2}=T
by definition.

Recalling that
\beta^{j}(x)=\beta(x)
unless
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\geq 2^{j/2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{t\in[0,T]}x^{i}(t)\neq\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t)})}=\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}x^{i}_{k}\neq\tilde{x}^{i,i}_{k}})}\leq\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}{\left\|\beta(x^{i}_{k})\right\|}\geq 2^{i/2}})}

We can bound
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{0}+L_{\beta}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}{(x,x_{0})}
, so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}{\left\|\beta(x^{i}_{k})\right\|}\geq 2^{i/2}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{k\in\left\{0...2^{i}\right\}}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}_{k})}\geq\frac{2^{i/2}-L_{0}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\exp{(2+8TL_{\beta}^{\prime}+TL_{R}d+TL_{R}L_{0}^{2})}\cdot{(2Td+4T^{2}L_{0}^{2})}\cdot{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\cdot 2^{-i+2}

where we use Lemma
26
, with
K=2^{i}
, and assume that
i
satisfies
2^{i/2}\geq L_{0}
and
2^{i}\geq T
.

Using identical steps, we can also bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{t\in[0,T]}x^{i+1}(t)\neq\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t)})}\leq\exp{(2+8TL_{\beta}^{\prime}+TL_{R}d+TL_{R}L_{0}^{2})}\cdot{(2Td+4T^{2}L_{0}^{2})}\cdot{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}\cdot 2^{-i+2}

Put together,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{({x}^{i}(t),{x}^{i+1}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}-2}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}-2}})}+\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{t\in[0,T]}x^{i}(t)\neq\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t)})}+\mathbb{P}{({\exists_{t\in[0,T]}x^{i+1}(t)\neq\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t)})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\cdot 2^{-i/2}

where
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
is a constant that depends on
T,L_{R},L_{\beta}^{\prime},L_{0},d
, but does not depend on
i
.

Step 2: Apply Borel-Cantelli to show uniformly-Cauchy sequence with probability 1

Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{i={\mathcal{C}}_{1}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}}})}<\infty

By the Borel-Cantelli Lemma,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}{({\sup_{t}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\geq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}}\text{ for infinitely many $i$}})}=0

Equivalently, with probability
1
, for all
\varepsilon
, there exists a
N
such that for all
i\geq N
,

\sup_{t}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\leq 2^{-\frac{i}{4}}
. For any
j\geq i\geq N
, it then follows that

\displaystyle\sup_{t}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{j}(t))}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=i}^{j}\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x^{\ell+1}(t))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=i}^{j}2^{-\frac{\ell}{4}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 6\cdot 2^{-i/4}

Step 3: Uniform-Cauchy sequence implies uniform convergence to limit using standard arguments

Therefore, with probability 1,
x^{i}(t)
is a uniformly Cauchy sequence. Let
x(t)
be the point-wise limit of
x^{i}(t)
, as
i\to\infty
. It follows
1
1

1

A nice clean proof can be seen at
https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/1287669/uniformly-cauchy-sequences
that with probability 1, for any
\varepsilon
, there exists a
N
such that for all
i\geq N
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x(t))}\leq\varepsilon

∎

A.2
\Phi
is an SDE

Proof of Lemma
2
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
denote the sigma field generated by
\mathbf{B}(s):s\in[0,t]
.

Consider any
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
with
{\left\|f^{\prime}\right\|}\leq C
,
{\left\|f^{\prime\prime}\right\|}\leq C
,
{\left\|f^{\prime\prime\prime}\right\|}\leq C
globally. Let
x^{i}(t)=\overline{\Phi}(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B},i)
be as defined in (
3.3
). We will verify that
f(x(t))-f(x(0))-\int_{0}^{t}Lf(x(t))dt
is a martingale.

To begin, let
s,t\in[0,T]
be such that
s=j\delta^{a}
and
t=j^{\prime}\delta^{a}
for some positive integers
j\leq j^{\prime},a
, where
\delta^{i}=T/2^{i}
. We will show that conditioned on
x(s)
,
f(x(t))-f(x(s))-\int_{s}^{t}Lf(x(t))dt
is a martingale. Let us define

\displaystyle u^{i}_{k}=\delta^{i}\beta(x^{i}_{k})+{(\mathbf{B}((k+1)\delta^{i})-\mathbf{B}(k\delta^{i}))}\circ E^{i}_{k}

so that
x^{i}(t)=\Exp_{x^{i}_{k}}{(\frac{t-k\delta^{i}}{\delta^{i}}u^{i}_{k})}
, where
t\in[k\delta^{i},(k+1)\delta^{i}]
.

Consider an arbitrary
\ell\geq a
. Consider the sum

\displaystyle\sum_{k=s/\delta^{i}}^{t/\delta^{i}-1}f(x^{\ell}((k+1)\delta^{\ell}))-f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))-\bm{\left\langle}u^{\ell}_{k},\nabla f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\bm{}-\nabla^{2}f(x^{\ell}_{k})[u^{\ell}_{k},u^{\ell}_{k}]

(A.8)

By Taylor’s theorem,

\displaystyle{\left|f(x^{\ell}((k+1)\delta^{\ell}))-f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))-\bm{\left\langle}u^{\ell}_{k},\nabla f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\bm{}-\nabla^{2}f(x^{\ell}_{k})[u^{\ell}_{k},u^{\ell}_{k}]\right|}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C{\left\|u^{\ell}_{k}\right\|}^{3}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 8C^{3}{\delta^{\ell}}^{3}{\left\|\beta(x^{\ell}_{k})\right\|}^{3}+8C^{3}{\left\|\mathbf{B}((k+1)\delta^{\ell})-\mathbf{B}(k\delta^{\ell})\right\|}_{2}^{3}

Notice also that

\displaystyle\bm{\left\langle}u^{\ell}_{k},\nabla f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\bm{}=

\displaystyle\delta^{\ell}\underbrace{\bm{\left\langle}\beta(x^{\ell}),\nabla f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\bm{}}_{{\left\|\cdot\right\|}^{2}\leq{\delta^{\ell}}^{2}C^{2}{\left\|\beta(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\right\|}}+\underbrace{\bm{\left\langle}\nabla f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell})),{(\mathbf{B}((k+1)\delta^{\ell})-\mathbf{B}(k\delta^{\ell}))}\circ E^{\ell}_{k}\bm{}}_{\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k\delta^{\ell}}}}\left[{\cdot}\right]=0}

and that
\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{k\delta^{\ell}}}}\left[{\nabla^{2}f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))[u^{\ell}_{k},u^{\ell}_{k}]-\delta^{\ell}\Delta f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))}\right]=0
, and that

\displaystyle{\left|\nabla^{2}f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))[u^{\ell}_{k},u^{\ell}_{k}]-\delta^{\ell}\Delta f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\right|}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle{\delta^{\ell}}^{4}C^{2}{\left\|\beta(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\right\|}^{4}

\displaystyle\qquad+{\delta^{\ell}}^{2}{\left\|\mathbf{B}((k+1)\delta^{\ell})-\mathbf{B}(k\delta^{\ell})\right\|}_{2}^{4}+{\delta^{\ell}}^{2}C^{2}d

Finally, note that there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
, which depends on
T,d,L_{\beta}^{\prime}{\left\|\beta(x_{0})\right\|}
, such that for all
\ell
, for all
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathbb{E}\left[{{\left\|\beta(x^{\ell}(t)\right\|}^{6}}\right]\leq C^{\prime}
. The proof is similar to Lemma
27
and we omit it here.

Plugging into (
A.8
) and taking expectation conditioned on
\mathbf{B}(t):t\in[0,s]
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{s}}}\left[{{\left|f(x^{\ell}(t))-f(x^{\ell}(s))+\sum_{k=s/\delta^{\ell}}^{t/\delta^{\ell}-1}-\delta^{\ell}\bm{\left\langle}\beta(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell})),\nabla f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\bm{}-\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{2}\Delta f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\right|}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{s}}}\left[{{\left|\sum_{k=s/\delta^{\ell}}^{t/\delta^{\ell}-1}f(x^{\ell}((k+1)\delta^{\ell}))-f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))-\delta^{\ell}\bm{\left\langle}\beta(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell})),\nabla f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\bm{}-\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{2}\Delta f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\right|}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle poly(C,C^{\prime},d)\sum_{k=s/\delta^{\ell}}^{t/\delta^{\ell}-1}{\delta^{\ell}}^{2}\leq poly(C,C^{\prime},d,T){\delta^{\ell}}

Next, define
g^{\ell}_{k}:=\delta^{\ell}\bm{\left\langle}\beta(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell})),\nabla f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))\bm{}+\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{2}\Delta f(x^{\ell}(k\delta^{\ell}))
. Using the smoothness of
\beta
and
f
, and the fact that
\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x(k\delta^{\ell}))}\leq\delta^{\ell}{\left\|\beta(x(k\delta^{\ell}))\right\|}+{\left\|\mathbf{B}((k+1)\delta^{\ell})-\mathbf{B}(k\delta^{\ell})\right\|}_{2}
, we verify that for any
k
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{s}}}\left[{{\left|g^{\ell}_{k}-\int_{k\delta^{\ell}}^{(k+1)\delta^{\ell}}\bm{\left\langle}\beta(x(r)),\nabla f(x(r))\bm{}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta f(x(r))dr\right|}}\right]\leq poly{(C,C^{\prime},d)}{\delta^{\ell}}^{3/2}

Putting everything together, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{s}}}\left[{{\left|f(x^{\ell}(t))-f(x^{\ell}(s))+\int_{s}^{t}-\bm{\left\langle}\beta(x^{\ell}(r)),\nabla f(x^{\ell}(r))\bm{}-\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{2}\Delta f(x^{\ell}(r))dr\right|}}\right]\leq poly(C,C^{\prime},d,T){\delta^{\ell}}^{1/2}

By Lemma
1
,
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x(t))}
converges to
0
almost surely as
\ell\to\infty
. By Dominated Convergence Theorem, and by smoothness of
f
and
\beta
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{s}}}\left[{{\left|f(x^{\ell}(t))-f(x^{\ell}(s))+\int_{s}^{t}-\bm{\left\langle}\beta(x^{\ell}(r)),\nabla f(x^{\ell}(r))\bm{}-\frac{\delta^{\ell}}{2}\Delta f(x^{\ell}(r))dr\right|}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{s}}}\left[{{\left|f(x(t))-f(x(s))+\int_{s}^{t}-\bm{\left\langle}\beta(x(r)),\nabla f(x(r))\bm{}-\frac{\delta}{2}\Delta f(x(r))dr\right|}}\right]=0

The
=0
is because
\lim_{\ell\to 0}poly(C,C^{\prime},d,T){\delta^{\ell}}^{1/2}=0
.

Recall that we assumed that
s
and
t
are integral multiples of
T/2^{a}
for some positive integer
a
. To extend to the case of general
s,t
, we can define
s^{\ell}:=\lfloor\frac{s}{\delta^{\ell}}\rfloor
and
\lfloor\frac{t}{\delta^{\ell}}\rfloor
. We know that
\mathbb{E}_{{{\mathcal{F}}_{s^{\ell}}}}\left[{{\left|f(x(t))-f(x(s))+\int_{s^{\ell}}^{t^{\ell}}-\bm{\left\langle}\beta(x(r)),\nabla f(x(r))\bm{}-\frac{\delta}{2}\Delta f(x(r))dr\right|}}\right]=0
for all
\ell
, then take the limit of
\ell to\infty
.

∎

A.3
Langevin MCMC on Manifold

Lemma 8
.

Let
\beta(\cdot)
be a vector field satisfying Assumption
2
. Assume also that there exists
L_{\beta}
such that
{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta}
for all
x
. Consider arbitrary
x_{0}\in M
and let
E
be an orthonormal basis of
T_{x_{0}}M
. Let
\mathbf{B}
be a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
x^{i}(t)=\overline{\Phi}(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B},i)
and
x(t)=\Phi(t;x,E,\beta,\mathbf{B})
as defined in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) respectively. (Existence of
x(t)
follows from Lemma
1
).

Then for any non-negative integer
\ell
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x(t))}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle 2^{14}e^{40T\delta^{\ell}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+2TL_{R}d+TL_{\beta}^{\prime}}T^{3}{({\delta^{\ell}}^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{\ell}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-\ell}

where
\delta^{i}:=2^{-i}T

Proof.

Consider any fixed
i
, let
\delta^{i}:=T/2^{i}
and let
K:=T/\delta^{i}=2^{i}
as in (
3.1
).

Following the same steps leading up to (
A.6
), we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{40K{\delta^{i}}^{2}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+2K\delta^{i}L_{R}d+K\delta^{i}L_{\beta}^{\prime}}{(K{\delta^{i}})}^{2}{({\delta^{i}}^{4}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+{\delta^{i}}L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{i}}^{2}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{\delta^{i}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{10}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{2^{10}e^{40T\delta^{i}L_{R}L_{\beta}^{2}+2TL_{R}d+TL_{\beta}^{\prime}}T^{3}{({\delta^{i}}^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{i}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}}_{:=s_{i}}\cdot 2^{-i}

where we use the fact that
K\delta^{i}=T
by definition.

By repeated application of Young’s Inequality, we can bound, for any
\ell
and any
j\geq\ell
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x^{j}(t))}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\ell}^{j-1}3{(\frac{3}{2})}^{i-\ell}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=\ell}^{j-1}3{(\frac{3}{2})}^{i-\ell}\cdot 2^{-i}s_{i}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 12\cdot 2^{-\ell}\cdot s_{\ell}

Since the above holds for any
j
, we can take the limit of
j\to\infty
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{\ell}(t),x(t))}^{2}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle 12\cdot 2^{-\ell}\cdot s_{\ell}

where we use the fact that
\mathrm{d}{(x^{j}_{2^{j}},x(T))}
converges almost surely to
0
, from Lemma
1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4
.

Let us define

\displaystyle\beta^{j}(x):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\beta(x)&\text{for }{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}\leq L_{1}2^{j/2+1}\\
\beta(x)\cdot\frac{L_{1}2^{j/2+1}}{{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}}&\text{for }{\left\|\beta(x)\right\|}>L_{1}2^{j/2+1}\end{array}\right.

Let us also define
L_{\beta^{j}}:=L_{1}2^{j/2}
. We verify that
{\left\|\beta^{j}(x)\right\|}\leq L_{\beta^{j}}
for all
x
.

Let
\tilde{x}^{i,j}(t):=\overline{\Phi}(t;x,E,\beta^{j},\mathbf{B},i)
and
\tilde{x}^{\cdot,j}(t):=\overline{\Phi}(t;x,E,\beta^{i},\mathbf{B},i)
. By Young’s Inequality,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),x^{i+1}(t))}\leq

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(t),\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t))}+\mathrm{d}{(x^{i+1}(t),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t))}+\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(t),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(t))}

(A.9)

We first bound the last term. Let
\delta^{i}:=T/2^{i}
. Using identical argument as Lemma
8
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T),\tilde{x}^{i+1,i}(T))}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{10}e^{40T\delta^{i}L_{R}L_{\beta^{i}}^{2}+2TL_{R}d+TL_{\beta}^{\prime}}T^{3}{({\delta^{i}}^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{\beta^{i}}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+{\delta^{i}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{\beta^{i}}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{10}e^{40T^{2}L_{R}L_{1}^{2}+2TL_{R}d+TL_{\beta}^{\prime}}T^{3}{(T^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{1}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+T{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{1}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}\cdot 2^{-i}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{14-i}T^{3}{(T^{3}L_{R}^{2}L_{1}^{6}+L_{R}^{2}d^{3}+T{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}L_{1}^{2}+{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}^{2}d)}

On the other hand, by triangle inequality and by Assumption
2
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T))}\leq

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(s),x_{0})}>\frac{2^{i/2+1}L_{1}-L_{0}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}\right\}}{(\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),x_{0})}+\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T),x_{0})})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(s),x_{0})}>\frac{2^{i/2}L_{1}}{L_{\beta}^{\prime}}\right\}}{(\mathrm{d}{(x^{i}(T),x_{0})}+\mathrm{d}{(\tilde{x}^{i,i}(T),x_{0})})}

Define
x^{i}_{k}:=x^{i}(k\delta^{i})
. Using the fact that by definition of (
3.3
),
x^{i}(t)
are linear interpolations of
x^{i}_{k}
for
k=0...2^{i}
,
